Minimax and Bayes Optimal Best-Arm Identification
Masahiro Kato∗
The University of Tokyo

arXiv:2506.24007v6 [econ.EM] 25 Jul 2026

July 28, 2026

Abstract
This study investigates minimax and Bayes optimal strategies for fixed-budget
best-arm identification. We consider an adaptive procedure consisting of a sampling
phase followed by a recommendation phase. Within this framework, we design an
adaptive experiment to efficiently identify the best arm, defined as the one with the
highest expected outcome. In our proposed strategy, the sampling phase consists of two
stages. The first stage is a pilot phase, in which we allocate samples uniformly across
arms to eliminate clearly suboptimal arms and to estimate outcome variances. Before
entering the second stage, we solve a Gaussian minimax game, which yields a sampling
policy and a decision rule. In the second stage, samples are allocated according to
this policy. After the sampling phase, the procedure enters the recommendation phase,
where we select an arm using the decision rule. We prove that this single strategy is
simultaneously asymptotically minimax and Bayes optimal for the simple regret, and
we establish upper bounds that coincide exactly with our lower bounds, including the
constant terms. The lower bounds hold against every adaptive experiment and for every
fixed number of arms, and the strategy attains them without knowing the outcome
distributions or the prior.

Keywords: Best-arm identification; minimax optimality; Bayes optimality; Neyman allocation; multi-armed bandits; adaptive experimental design; machine learning; econometrics;
statistics; treatment choice

1

Introduction

We investigate the problem of fixed-budget best-arm identification (BAI, Audibert et al.,
2010), an instance of adaptive experimental design for identifying the arm with the highest
expected outcome. This problem is also known by various names across disciplines, including
ordinal optimization (Chen et al., 2000) and adaptive experimental design for policy choice
(Kasy & Sautmann, 2021).
∗

Email: mkato-csecon@g.ecc.u-tokyo.ac.jp

1

An adaptive experimental algorithm in BAI usually consists of two phases: the sampling
phase and the recommendation phase (Kaufmann et al., 2016). Given a total of T rounds,
the strategy selects one arm at each round using the observations collected so far. After the
final round, it recommends an arm using all collected observations.
In this study, we propose an asymptotically minimax and Bayes optimal strategy for BAI.
First, we determine the exact asymptotic minimax and Bayes lower bounds for the simple
regret for every fixed finite number of arms K ≥ 2 over the class of all adaptive experiments.
The outcomes follow mean-parameterized canonical exponential families on a compact mean
interval under the structural and moment conditions in Section 3. The constant in the
minimax lower bound is Γ (P ), which is characterized
by sequential Gaussian games. For

K
distinct arms a and b, the pairwise tie set is µ ∈ M : µa = µb = maxc∈[K] µc . It consists
of the mean vectors for which arms a and b have the same largest mean. For every prior
satisfying Assumption 7.1, the constant in the Bayes lower bound is determined by the prior
density on these pairwise tie sets. In the Bernoulli case, the matching lower bound establishes
the optimality of the leading constant conjectured in Komiyama et al. (2023, Eq. (6)).
We refer to our proposed strategy as Sequential Minimax Allocation with Screening
(SMAS). This single strategy attains both minimax and Bayes lower bounds with matching
constants. SMAS begins with a pilot phase whose fraction of the total budget converges to
zero as T → ∞. This phase removes clearly suboptimal arms and estimates the outcome
variances. When at least three arms remain, SMAS uses a sequential allocation rule that is
updated after every observation. When two arms remain, it uses plug-in Neyman allocation.
The sampling proportions are proportional to the estimated standard deviations. The strategy
uses the seven constants in Assumption 3.1 that give common variance bounds, a common
derivative bound, and common constants in the two exponential-moment conditions. It is not
supplied with the families of outcome distributions for the individual arms or their variance
functions. It estimates the variances needed for allocation from the pilot observations.

1.1

Setup

We formulate the problem as follows. There are K arms, and each arm a ∈ [K] :=
{1, 2, . . . , K} has a potential outcome Ya ∈ Y, where Y ⊆ R denotes the outcome space. Each
potential outcome Ya follows a (marginal) distribution Pa,µa parameterized by µa ∈ M, where
M ⊂ R is a parameter space. For the parameter vector µ := (µ1 , µ2 , . . . , µK ) ∈ MK , let
Pµ := (P1,µ1 , P2,µ2 , . . . , PK,µK ) be the bandit instance at µ, the K-tuple of arm distributions.
The parameter µa := EPa,µa [Ya ] is the mean of arm a.
Whenever an arm-valued arg max or arg min is not unique, we choose the arm with the
smallest index.
Under a bandit instance Pµ , our objective is to identify the best arm
a∗µ = arg max µa ,
a∈[K]

through an adaptive experiment in which observations are collected from Pµ according to
our strategy.

Assume there exist i.i.d. random vectors (Y1,t , . . . , YK,t )t∈[T ] such that for each a ∈ [K] and
each t ∈ [T ], Ya,t ∼ Pa,µa . We consider an adaptive experimental procedure consisting of two
phases:
1. Sampling phase: For each t ∈ [T ] := {1, 2, . . . , T }:
• An arm At ∈ [K] is selected based on the past observations {(As , Ys )}t−1

observed outcomes {(At , Yt )}Tt=1 , we recommend an arm a

best arm aµ .
Our task is to design a strategy δ that determines how arms are selected during the
sampling phase and how the final arm is recommended. We define a strategy through
the conditional laws governing the sampling decisions and the final recommendation. This
definition includes randomized rules.
Throughout this study, we equip [K] with the power-set sigma-algebra 2[K] and Y ⊆ R
with its Borel sigma-algebra. For each t ≥ 0, let Ht := ([K] × Y)t be equipped with the
corresponding product sigma-algebra, where H0 consists of the empty history. A probability
kernel from a measurable space H to [K] assigns a probability distribution on [K] to each
h ∈ H. This distribution varies measurably with h.
Definition 1.1 (Strategy and the class of all adaptive experiments). Fix a budget T ∈ N. A
strategy δT consists of sampling kernels Qt,T from Ht−1 to [K] for t ∈ [T ] and a recommendation
kernel Qrec
T from HT to [K]. Given the observed history ht−1 ∈ Ht−1 , the strategy selects the
δ
arm At according to Qt,T (ht−1 , ·). Given the full history hT ∈ HT , it recommends the arm
bδT according to Qrec
a
T (h
QT , ·). Let AT denote the class of all such strategies, with no further
restrictions. Let A := T ∈N AT denote the class of all adaptive experiments, whose elements
are sequences δ = (δT )T ∈N .
Every strategy in AT can be represented without changing its sampling or recommendation
kernels: its sampling actions and final recommendation are measurable functions of the
observed history and a random element U generated before the experiment and independent
of the outcomes. Lemma L.1 gives the construction.
Throughout this study, a strategy is a complete adaptive experiment in the original bandit
model. It includes all sampling kernels and the final recommendation kernel. A policy is a
rule used in an auxiliary sequential game or in the main phase of SMAS. At each round, a
policy specifies a distribution over the available arms as a function of the observed history or
state. After the policy’s last round, its terminal kernel assigns a probability distribution to
the recommended arm.
The class AT contains every nonadaptive, plug-in, and fully sequential design at budget
T . A deterministic rule is the special case in which every kernel assigns probability one to a
single arm at each history. For simplicity, we omit the dependence on δ when it is clear from
the context.

1.2

Main Results

We evaluate δ SMAS under the minimax and Bayes regret criteria. The minimax criterion takes
the worst case over µ ∈ MK for a fixed collection P , consisting of one family of outcome
distributions for each arm. The Bayes criterion averages the regret over the same mean space
under a prior. The collection P is held fixed in both criteria.
For every fixed finite number of arms K ≥ 2, we develop a strategy that attains the exact
asymptotic simple-regret constants over all adaptive experiments under both the minimax
and Bayes criteria. We first derive asymptotic minimax and Bayes lower bounds for each
fixed collection of mean-parameterized canonical exponential families satisfying Definition 3.1.
The upper bounds additionally use Assumption 3.1 to construct one strategy for the model
class. The lower bounds hold over all adaptive experiments, including fully sequential designs,
and δ SMAS attains both constants.
Sequential Minimax Allocation with Screening (SMAS), denoted by δ SMAS , begins with
a pilot phase whose fraction of the total budget converges to zero as T → ∞. This phase
removes clearly suboptimal arms and estimates the outcome variances. When at least three
arms remain, it uses a sequential allocation rule that is updated after every observation. When
4

two arms remain, it uses plug-in Neyman allocation. The strategy also reserves observations
for an independent final comparison. It uses only the seven constants v, v, Lv , λ0 , C0 , η0 ,
and D0 specified in Assumption 3.1. It does not use the families of outcome distributions for
the individual arms, their variance functions, or the prior. Matching lower and upper bounds
establish exact asymptotic minimax and Bayes optimality for every fixed finite number of
arms.
The exact minimax constant Γ (P ) is defined through the sequential Gaussian games in
Section 4. The exact Bayes constant is defined in Section 7.1 through the density of the prior
on the pairwise tie sets.
Summary of main theoretical results To illustrate the main results, suppose that Ya
follows a Gaussian distribution with mean µa and variance σa2 . Let P denote the collection
of the K families of outcome distributions, and let B (P ) be the bandit model obtained by
varying µ ∈ MK while holding those families fixed. In this Gaussian illustration, σa2 (µ) = σa2 .
The general model in Section 3 also allows mean-dependent variances and includes Bernoulli
outcomes on a compact subinterval of (0, 1).
We define the minimax and Bayes regret as follows:
• Minimax regret: supµ∈MK RegretδT (µ).
R
• Bayes regret: µ∈MK RegretδT (µ)dH(µ), where H is a prior distribution on MK with
a bounded and uniformly continuous density.
We evaluate both criteria over the class of all adaptive experiments. For every fixed finite
number of arms, the same strategy attains the corresponding leading constants under the
minimax and Bayes criteria. Here, Bayes optimality refers to the integrated frequentist risk
under each admissible prior. SMAS itself does not use the prior.
Asymptotic minimax optimality We show that δ SMAS is asymptotically minimax optimal:

lower bound holds over every adaptive experiment, and δ SMAS ∈ A attains it.
For K = 2, the constant has the closed form Γ(P ) = supµ∈M (σ1 (µ) + σ2 (µ)) cmm . The
corresponding binary Gaussian game is attained asymptotically by the deterministic Neyman
allocation. SMAS implements this allocation using the pilot estimates of the standard
deviations. For K ≥ 3, Γ (P ) is defined by the sequential Gaussian game in Section 4. This
is an exact characterization of the constant rather than a closed-form formula.

where C Bayes (P , H) is the exact Bayes constant defined in Section 7.1, and the infimum is
over the class A of all adaptive experiments. Thus, as T → ∞, the scaled Bayes regret of
SMAS converges to the exact Bayes constant C Bayes (P , H) (Corollary 7.3), and the same
strategy attains the exact constants in both criteria.
The competing experiments may depend on P . Under the Bayes criterion, they may also
depend on H. They may not depend on the unknown mean vector µ. SMAS depends only
on K, M, and the seven constants collected in E in Assumption 3.1. SMAS uses the same
tuning throughout the model class and for all admissible priors. The first convergence is
pointwise in P , and the second is pointwise in (P , H). Corollary 7.4 states the resulting
quantifier order.
Main message Minimax regret is governed by near ties with gaps of order T −1/2 , whereas
Bayes regret under regular priors is governed by pairwise near ties between the best and
second-best arms. Although the two criteria are determined by different local mean vectors,
one strategy attains both exact constants.
Organization The remainder of this study is organized as follows. In Section 2, we review
related work. Section 3 introduces a class of distributions that we consider. Section 4
describes the sequential Gaussian game, and Section 5 defines our strategy. Section 6 treats
the minimax criterion and Section 7 the Bayes criterion, each presenting a lower bound and
a matching upper bound. Section 8 states the main steps of the four bounds and the order in
which the limits are taken. In Section 9, we discuss related problems.

2

Literature Review

This section reviews the criteria and results most closely related to this study. For a more
detailed review, see Appendix B. The earliest BAI formulations appeared under the name
ordinal optimization (Chen et al., 2000; Glynn & Juneja, 2004), with an emphasis on nonadaptive sampling proportions and large-deviation criteria. Those analyses often characterize
an optimal vector of sampling proportions using the outcome distributions of the arms. Later
work incorporated the estimation of the target sampling proportions into the experimental
design (Audibert et al., 2010; Bubeck et al., 2011).
BAI is mainly studied in the fixed-confidence and fixed-budget settings (Bubeck et al.,
2011; Audibert et al., 2010; Kaufmann et al., 2016). In the fixed-confidence setting, the
error probability is controlled at a prescribed level and the sample size is minimized. In the
fixed-budget setting, the budget is fixed in advance and the objective is to minimize the
probability of misidentification or the simple regret. Exact instance-dependent results are
well developed in the fixed-confidence setting. In the fixed-budget setting, the budget is
fixed before the observations are collected, and its optimal error rate remains less completely
characterized (Kaufmann, 2020).

6

A central question in fixed-budget BAI is whether one strategy can attain the best
distribution-dependent error exponent at every instance. Garivier & Kaufmann (2016)
conjectures an information-theoretic benchmark for the probability of misidentification
Pµ a
bδT ̸= a∗µ , but no strategy is known to match it at every instance. This difficulty is closely
related to the reverse Kullback–Leibler divergence problem discussed by Kaufmann (2020).
Barrier et al. (2023) develops upper and lower bounds on the average log-probability of
misidentification for general distribution classes. Its distribution class includes all distributions
on a bounded interval. The criterion in that study is the exponential scale of misidentification,
whereas we study the leading constant of expected simple regret under minimax and Bayes
criteria.
Ariu et al. (2021) gives an earlier impossibility result for distribution-dependent expected
policy regret. Using the construction of Carpentier & Locatelli (2016), the result shows
that, for any algorithm, there is a problem instance and an infinite subsequence of budgets
along which the expected policy regret is slower than the oracle rate claimed by Kasy &
Sautmann (2021). This lower bound provides the counterexample that refutes the claimed
global optimality of exploration sampling. Degenne (2023) later formulates uniform attainability through the notion of a complexity and proves nonexistence for several fixed-budget
identification tasks, including two-armed Bernoulli BAI. The BAI impossibility result in Ariu
et al. (2021) predates this general formulation. The contribution of Degenne (2023) is the
definition of complexity and its extension to a broader class of identification tasks. Wang
et al. (2024) further shows that, in two-armed Bernoulli A/B testing, no algorithm weakly
improves on the fixed-instance exponent of uniform sampling at every instance and strictly
improves on it at some instance. Goldberger (2026) gives a related impossibility result for
fixed-instance error exponents; that criterion differs from the minimax and Bayes expected
simple regret studied here.
Fixed-instance analyses also contain positive dominance results. Imbens et al. (2025)
shows that batched elimination can strictly dominate completely randomized trials when there
are at least three homoscedastic Gaussian arms. Wang et al. (2023) studies fixed-budget BAI
from a large-deviation perspective, and Balagopalan et al. (2026) relates fixed-budget and
fixed-confidence guarantees up to logarithmic factors. These studies concern fixed-instance
performance on an exponential scale.
At a fixed µ with a unique best arm and positive gaps, the probability of misidentification and the simple regret have the same logarithmic decay rate. Writing ∆min (µ) =
mina̸=a∗µ ∆µ (a) > 0 and ∆max (µ) = maxa∈[K] ∆µ (a) < ∞, neither of which depends on T , the

P
identity RegretδT (µ) = a∈[K] ∆µ (a)Pµ a
bδT = a gives


bδT ̸= a∗µ ≤ RegretδT (µ) ≤ ∆max (µ)Pµ a

Taking T −1 log gives the same limit inferior and the same limit superior for the two quantities.
Under minimax and Bayes criteria, the relevant gaps may shrink with T . The gap factor then
contributes to the first-order risk, and the probability of misidentification and the simple
regret have different leading behavior.
Subsequent work studies minimax and Bayes analyses, which avoid the pointwise attainability problem by changing the quantifier order. In the minimax criterion, the worst-case
mean vector may depend on the budget. Under a prior, neighborhoods of the pairwise tie
7

sets retain enough prior mass to determine the leading Bayes risk. The resulting expected
simple regret is polynomial rather than exponential in T . Within the exponent criterion,
Komiyama et al. (2022) studies a normalized minimax error exponent over fixed instances.
That result remains on the exponential scale and addresses a different asymptotic quantity
from the T −1/2 minimax simple regret studied here.
For expected simple regret, Bubeck et al. (2011) establishes the minimax rate. Komiyama
et al. (2023) establishes the Bayes rate for Bernoulli bandits under regular priors and gives
an explicit 1/T upper bound with a leading constant conjectured to be optimal. Atsidakou
et al. (2022) and Nguyen et al. (2025) also study Bayesian fixed-budget BAI, including
prior-dependent sampling proportions and structured bandit models. In the Bernoulli case,
our exact Bayes constant coincides with the constant in Komiyama et al. (2023, Eq. (6)), and
the matching lower bound establishes its optimality. Our model also allows heteroscedastic
outcomes in mean-parameterized canonical exponential families.
Several studies obtain sharper minimax results under local or restricted formulations.
Adusumilli (2022) and Adusumilli (2023) derive exact two-arm minimax results in local
diffusion-limit formulations, where the Neyman allocation is optimal. Kato (2024) obtains
matching lower and upper bounds in a small-gap formulation under a consistency condition on
the competing strategies. Kato et al. (2023) derives variance-dependent minimax simple-regret
bounds under a local location-shift model and proposes a design attaining the T −1/2 rate,
although its lower and upper leading constants do not coincide. These studies connect local
BAI with Gaussian experiments and variance-dependent arm selection.
For two arms, our minimax constant also reduces to the Neyman value. The present
analysis takes the worst case over the global compact mean space and compares SMAS with
all adaptive experiments. The analysis also allows the variance functions in the exponentialfamily model to depend on the mean. For three or more arms, the exact minimax constant
is characterized by the sequential Gaussian game, whose sampling rule may respond to the
observations. This characterization allows the lower and upper bounds to use the same value
for every fixed finite number of arms.
We establish matching minimax and Bayes lower and upper bounds, including the
leading constants, for every fixed finite number of arms. The loss is the expected simple
regret. The outcomes follow mean-parameterized canonical exponential families satisfying
the structural and moment conditions in Section 3, and the Bayes result applies to the priors
in Assumption 7.1. Both lower bounds range over all adaptive experiments. The competing
strategies may use the families of outcome distributions for the arms and, under the Bayes
criterion, the prior. SMAS is constructed using only K, M, and the seven constants in E
defined by Assumption 3.1. It uses neither those families nor the prior. A single strategy
attains the minimax T −1/2 constant and the Bayes T −1 constant.
Our proofs use change-of-measure arguments and LAN (Le Cam, 1972, 1986). Changeof-measure identities also underlie distribution-dependent analyses of cumulative reward
maximization (Lai & Robbins, 1985) and fixed-confidence BAI (Garivier & Kaufmann, 2016;
Kaufmann et al., 2016). Related work uses LAN to analyze adaptive experiments (Hirano &
Porter, 2025; Adusumilli, 2022, 2023, 2025; Armstrong, 2022). However, those studies first
restrict the parameters to local alternatives. Such analyses do not by themselves control
mean vectors with nonlocal gaps or eliminate suboptimal arms. In fixed-budget BAI, their
exact optimality results are confined to two-arm problems or restricted classes of strategies.

Table 1 places our results beside representative results for fixed-budget BAI and cumulative
reward maximization (CRM). The columns refer to the criterion used within each problem.
The BAI distribution-dependent entry concerns fixed-instance performance, whereas the
minimax and Bayes entries concern expected simple regret. The CRM entries concern
cumulative regret.
For BAI, the table separates impossibility results for uniform distribution-dependent
optimality from exact minimax and Bayes results for expected simple regret. The CRM
results provide earlier examples of exact asymptotic analysis for adaptive arm selection under
distribution-dependent and Bayes criteria, although the loss is cumulative regret rather than
terminal simple regret.

3

Bandit Models

From this section onward, we analyze the minimax and Bayes optimality of SMAS against
the competing experiments specified in Sections 6 and 7. We first define a class P of outcome
distributions and a collection P of arm families belonging to this class. We then define the
bandit model B (P ) induced by P and evaluate worst-case and average-case performance
over its mean vectors.
We consider canonical exponential families for this class, which are typically defined as
follows (Garivier & Kaufmann, 2016):



dPθ
(Pθ )θ∈Θ :
(y) = exp yθ − b θ
.
dξ
Here, Pθ is indexed by the natural parameter θ, whereas Pµ elsewhere is indexed by the mean.
The set Θ ⊂ R is the natural-parameter space. The measure ξ is a reference measure on Y,
and b : Θ → R is a convex and twice differentiable function.
In this study, however, we evaluate worst-case and average-case performance over the
mean parameter and express the lower and upper bounds in terms of variances. The mean
equals ḃ(θ), and the variance equals b̈(θ). We therefore define the distribution class in terms
of the mean and variance. This section provides such a definition, introduces a collection
of K arm families, and defines the bandit model that the collection induces by varying the
mean vector.

9

3.1

Mean-Parameterized Canonical Exponential Families

We define a class of mean-parameterized canonical exponential families. This class is essentially
the standard canonical exponential family, but the present definition serves three purposes: (i)
it parameterizes the distribution class by the mean, (ii) it makes the inverse Fisher information
equal to the variance, and (iii) it guarantees the finite third moments required in our analysis.
Common distributions such as Gaussian and Bernoulli are included in this class.
Definition 3.1 (Mean-parameterized canonical exponential family). Fix a measurable outcome space (Y, A ) and a compact interval M ⊂ R. For each arm a ∈ [K], let ξa be a σ-finite
measure on (Y, A ) and let σa2 : M → (0, ∞) be twice continuously differentiable. We say
that {Pa,µ : µ ∈ M} belongs to P(σa2 , M, Y) if there exist an open interval Θa ⊂ R, a strictly
convex C 3 log-partition function ba : Θa → R, and a C 1 map θa : M → Θa such that for all
µ ∈ M:
(y) = exp{yθa (µ) − ba (θa (µ))};

3. b̈a (θa (µ)) = σa2 (µ);

Remark. The outcome space Y and the parameter space M should be chosen to satisfy the
conditions in Definition 3.1. For example, if the outcome Ya follows a Bernoulli distribution
with support Y = {0, 1}, we can choose M = [µ, µ] ⊂ (0, 1) with 0 < µ < µ < 1. If we instead
choose M = [0, 1], the variance equals 0 at µ = 0 and µ = 1, which violates our definition of
the class. In this case, the Fisher information does not exist at µ = 0 and µ = 1, since the
1
Fisher information is given by I(µ) = µ(1−µ)
.

condition 5 yields uniform concentration of the pilot variance estimator in Lemma G.2. It
excludes some unbounded canonical exponential

used in the lower-bound proofs. The differentiability assumptions on the variance functions
10

support the Taylor expansion and the continuity arguments used in the minimax and Bayes
proofs. The lower-bound theorems are stated for each fixed collection satisfying Definition 3.1.
The upper-bound construction additionally uses the common bounds in Assumption 3.1.

3.2

Bandit Model

We distinguish a collection of arm families, the bandit model induced by that collection,
and the class of collections satisfying Assumption 3.1 with the same seven constants. The
minimax and Bayes criteria vary the mean vector within a fixed bandit model.
The first is a collection of arm families. For each arm a ∈ [K], let σa2 : M → (0, ∞) be twice
continuously differentiable and bounded away from 0 and ∞, and let Pa = {Pa,µ : µ ∈ M}
be a family in the class P(σa2 , M, Y) of Definition 3.1. We call Pa the family of outcome
distributions for arm a. We write

P = (Pa )a∈[K] ,
σ 2 = σa2 a∈[K] ,
and call P a collection of arm families with variance functions σ 2 . The collection P describes
the true environment, is fixed throughout an experiment, and is not supplied to the strategy.
The second is the bandit model that P induces. Recall that for a mean vector µ =
(µa )a∈[K] ∈ MK the bandit instance is Pµ = (Pa,µa )a∈[K] , in which arm a has mean µa . Define

B (P ) := Pµ : µ ∈ MK .
Only the mean vector varies within B (P ), while the arm families, and with them the variance
functions σ 2 , are held fixed. The worst case of Section 6 and the average of Section 7 are
both taken over µ ∈ MK , that is, over B (P ) at a fixed P . Neither a supremum nor an
average is taken over the choice of P .
Example (Bandit instances). The bandit model B (P ) allows heterogeneity across arms. For
example, with K = 2, arm 1 may be Bernoulli(µ1 ), so σ12 (µ) = µ(1 − µ), while arm 2 may be
Gaussian with possibly constant variance σ22 (µ) ≡ σ22 .
The third is the class of collections satisfying the same uniform bounds on the variance
functions and the same uniform moment conditions.
Assumption 3.1 (Uniform bounds on variance functions and exponential moments). There
are finite constants 0 < v < v < ∞, Lv < ∞, λ0 > 0, C0 < ∞, η0 > 0, and D0 < ∞ such
that, for every arm a ∈ [K] and every µ ∈ M,
v ≤ σa2 (µ) ≤ v,

∂µ σa2 (µ) ≤ Lv ,

and the moment inequalities (4) and (5) of Definition 3.1 hold with the common constants
(λ0 , C0 ) in place of (λ0,a , Ca ) and (η0 , D0 ) in place of (η0,a , Da ). We write
E := (v, v, Lv , λ0 , C0 , η0 , D0 ) .

(1)

Here, v and v are uniform lower and upper bounds on the variance functions σa2 (µ). The
constant Lv is a uniform upper bound on |∂µ σa2 (µ)|. The pair (λ0 , C0 ) specifies the range
|λ| ≤ λ0 and the upper bound exp (C0 λ2 ) in the local exponential-moment condition. The pair
(η0 , D0 ) specifies the range 0 ≤ η ≤ η0 and the upper bound D0 in the square-exponentialmoment condition.
11

Because M is compact and each σa2 is twice continuously differentiable and strictly
positive by Definition 3.1, each variance function is bounded above and away from zero on
M, and its derivative is bounded. Since K is finite, the arm-specific moment constants
in Definition 3.1 can also be replaced by common constants. Thus, for every collection P
satisfying Definition 3.1, there is at least one choice of the seven constants E for which
Assumption 3.1 holds.
Algorithm 1 is defined using one choice of the seven constants E satisfying Assumption 3.1.
Together with K and M, these constants determine the tolerances, the finite grid used to
round estimated standard deviations, and the budget thresholds. The seven constants need
not be the smallest valid bounds. For the finite calculations below, the endpoints of M may
be supplied through rational lower and upper bounds of any prescribed positive width. We
may also use smaller positive rational bounds for v, λ0 , and η0 , and larger rational bounds
for v, Lv , C0 , and D0 . Different valid choices of E may produce different tolerances, grids,
and budget thresholds, but they do not change Γ(P ) or C Bayes (P , H), which are defined
directly from P and H. The strategy does not use the collection P , the variance functions
σa2 (·), or their pointwise values.
Fixing the seven constants E determines the class of collections for which the strategy
uses the same tolerances, grids, and budget thresholds. By condition 3 of Definition 3.1, each
family Pa determines its variance function, σa2 (µ) = VarPa,µ (Y ) for µ ∈ M. Thus, a collection
P = (Pa )a∈[K] determines σ 2 = (σa2 )a∈[K] , and we define

Finite Sequential Gaussian Game

The exact minimax constant is built from finite sequential Gaussian games. In these discretetime experiments, the sampling rule may respond to every observation. The constant is not
12

the value of a single game. We first let the horizon tend to infinity. We then let the radius C
of the parameter set [−C, C]S tend to infinity and finally maximize over the common mean
m of the tied arms. We describe the game here and record its value. Appendices C and D
give the formal construction and all proofs.
Let S ⊆ [K] with |S| ≥ 2, let n ≥ |S|, and let s = (sa )a∈S ∈ (0, ∞)S be a vector of
standard deviations. Nature chooses a local parameter u = (ua )a∈S ∈ RS . At round t, a
randomized history-dependent policy selects an arm At ∈ S from a distribution determined
measurably by the preceding observations and auxiliary randomness independent of the
Gaussian noises. We refer to such a policy as a behavioral policy. Conditional on At = a, we
observe

incurs the simple regret ∆u (An ) = maxa∈S ua − uAbn . The policy may update the distribution
of At after every observation. This makes the sampling rule fully sequential.
For the parameter set [−C, C]S with C < ∞, the value GC

We define the exact minimax constant of the bandit model B (P ) induced by P by taking
the supremum of the game on all K arms over the common mean m of the tied arms:

Γ(P ) := sup G↑[K] (σa (m))a∈[K] .
m∈M

The finiteness of Γ(P ) follows from Definition D.1. The constant depends on P only through
the functions m 7→ σa (m) for a ∈ [K]. Thus, two collections in P (E) with the same
standard-deviation functions have the same exact minimax constant.
Least favorable instances are near ties, where the top means differ by O(T −1/2 ). The
competing arms therefore share a common limiting mean m, and their standard deviations
are evaluated at that mean. Screening may retain any subset of locally competitive arms. By
Lemma D.7, the game value does not decrease when arms are added. Hence, Γ(P ) dominates
the game value on every subset that screening may retain, as stated in Corollary D.9, and no
additional maximization over subsets is required.
For two arms, the value is available in closed form,
G↑{a,b} (sa , sb ) = (sa + sb ) cmm ,

cmm = sup xΦ(−x) ≈ 0.1699712,
x≥0

attained asymptotically by the deterministic Neyman allocation Na /n → sa /(sa + sb ) as
n → ∞, together with the empirical-best recommendation, and no sampling rule that adapts
to the observed outcomes improves the constant (Lemma D.6). For K = 2, this gives
Γ(P ) = supµ∈M (σ1 (µ) + σ2 (µ)) cmm .
For K ≥ 3, the exact constant is the value of the sequential game over all adaptive
experiments. The static value is at least as large because static policies form a subclass of
adaptive policies. Appendix A gives a three-arm example in which the inequality is strict.

5

Sequential Minimax Allocation with Screening

We now define Sequential Minimax Allocation with Screening (SMAS), denoted by δ SMAS .
The strategy attains Γ (P ) and C Bayes (P , H). It estimates the outcome variances from the
pilot sample and otherwise uses only K, M, and the constants in E defined in (1). Algorithm 1
summarizes the strategy, and Appendix G gives the full definition.
The strategy uses indices j ≥ 8. We set αj = γj = j −2 , Bj = j 2 , Hj = j 3 , Rj = 8j 2 , and
ωj = j −4 . Definition 5.1 fixes the remaining mesh and tolerance parameters at values in
(0, j −4 ]. For each j, the deterministic number Nj is chosen so that every sample-size and error
requirement attached to j holds for all T ≥ Nj ; we call Nj the budget threshold for j. The
thresholds increase to infinity and determine jT = max {j ≥ 8 : Nj ≤ T } for each budget T .
The calculation performed before data collection first fixes the finite-horizon approximation
and computes the policies and their derivative bounds. It then chooses the tolerances and the
finite grid used to round the estimated standard deviations before determining the budget
thresholds. Theorem G.4 shows that every step terminates using only K, M, and E.
Definition 5.1 (Indexed family of near-minimax policies). For each j and each arm subset

to the index.

policy assigned to the resulting index. Thus, no policy is computed while the experiment runs.

Asymptotic Bayes Optimality

This section establishes matching lower and upper bounds for the Bayes regret of SMAS.

7.1

Exact Bayes Constant

Let H be a prior distribution on MK satisfying the following condition.
Assumption 7.1 (Prior with a bounded uniformly continuous density). The prior H admits
a density h with respect to Lebesgue measure on MK , and h is bounded and uniformly
continuous on the compact set MK .

7.2

Asymptotic Bayes Lower Bound

We now show a Bayes lower bound. Let H be a prior distribution on MK . Every prior
integral below is taken against the density h of Assumption 7.1. In particular, the proof
of Theorem 7.1 evaluates the prior density at points satisfying µa = µb = m, written as
h(µ−ab , µa = m, µb = m). These points lie in MK , where h is defined and continuous.
Theorem 7.1 (Asymptotic Bayes lower bound). Assume the same outcome model as in
Theorem 6.1, and let the prior H satisfy Assumption 7.1. Then, for the class A of all adaptive
experiments,
Z
inf lim inf T

δ∈A T →∞

µ∈MK

RegretδT (µ) dH (µ) ≥ C Bayes (P , H) ,

where C Bayes (P , H) is given in (5).
The constant C Bayes (P , H) is governed by pairwise near ties between the best and secondbest arms. The factor σa (m) + σb (m) arises because the common mean m of the tied arms
is unknown and is averaged under the prior. The proof compares a local instance in which
arm a has the larger mean with a second local instance in which arm b has the larger mean.
The common mean in the second instance is shifted so that each selection of either arm
contributes the same standardized information. A change of variables in the integral over m
then maps the second comparison back to the original pair. A pointwise comparison at a
known m would have a different constant, as stated in Remark K.3.

7.3

Asymptotic Bayes Upper Bound

The lower bound allows competing experiments to depend on the arm families and the prior.
SMAS attains the same constant using only K, M, and the constants in E. Appendix H
gives the proof.

and Assumption 3.1, the deterministic budget thresholds and the policies indexed by j in

Proof sketch for Theorem 7.2 After multiplying the Bayes regret by T , only mean
vectors with a near tie between two arms contribute to the limit. On the event where the pilot
mean and variance estimates satisfy their concentration bounds, the set of mean vectors for
which at least three arms are retained has prior mass of order O (τT2 ), and their contribution
vanishes as T → ∞. The remaining
leading term

 comes from the two-arm plug-in Neyman
2

rule and yields the factor 14 σa (m) + σb∗\{a} (m) after integrating over the mean vectors on
which the two relevant arms have the same largest mean.
The policies used when at least three arms remain after screening are needed for the
minimax result. Under a prior satisfying Assumption 7.1, mean vectors with three or more
near-tied arms make a vanishing contribution after the Bayes regret is multiplied by T . The
Bayes constant is therefore determined by the two-arm sampling rule. A prior-averaged
bound controls the additional regret caused by the final comparison rule.
The Bayes lower bound in Theorem 7.1 and the matching upper bound in Theorem 7.2
imply asymptotic Bayes optimality.

8

Structure of the Proofs

This section summarizes the main steps of the proofs and the order of limits.
For the minimax lower bound, Lemma E.1 reduces the problem to a local S-arm experiment.
Lemmas E.2 and D.11 express the original and Gaussian experiments through posterior
21

Bellman recursions. Lemmas E.6, E.7, and E.8 then compare the two Bellman values.
Lemma C.3 provides a maximizing finite-grid prior, and Lemma D.10 and Theorem D.2
recover the sequential Gaussian-game constant. We first fix S, the common mean m, the
radius C, and the grid U, and then let T → ∞. After that limit, we send the grid mesh to
zero and the radius to infinity, and finally maximize over the common mean m of the tied
arms. The arm set remains fixed at S = [K].
The minimax and Bayes upper bounds use the same pilot phase and screening rule,
and both approximate the standard deviation of the scaled difference between the two
sample means. In the minimax proof, the strategy uses a policy from the indexed family in
Definition 5.1 when at least three arms remain and the two-arm sampling rule when two arms
remain. The Bayes proof instead shows that mean vectors with three or more near-tied arms
have negligible prior mass and analyzes the retained pair consisting of the two largest-mean
arms by averaging the two-arm risk bound under the prior. For each fixed j, the scaled
regret is bounded by the corresponding minimax or Bayes constant plus terms that vanish as
T → ∞ and terms that vanish as j → ∞. The budget thresholds (15) define the index jT
used at budget T , and all these error terms vanish along this sequence.
For the Bayes lower bound, we restrict the prior integral to neighborhoods of the pairwise
tie sets. Lemmas K.3 and K.4 yield the testing affinity used in Lemma K.6. We first take
T → ∞ with the cutoff U on the local displacement, the interval of possible common means,
and the remaining means fixed. We then extend that interval to M and let U → ∞.

9

Discussion

9.1

Minimax Optimal Strategy

The minimax
constant is determined by mean vectors for which the relevant gaps are of order

−1/2
O T
. Screening does not determine this local constant. In SMAS, it controls mean
vectors with nonvanishing gaps and connects the problem over the full compact mean space
to the sequential Gaussian game used in the minimax analysis.

9.2

Computing the Gaussian-Game Policy

The policies and budget thresholds in Definition 5.1 are computed before data collection.
Theorem D.5 gives a quantitative bound for the finite-horizon Gaussian game. This bound
determines a finite horizon for each index j. The parameter grids and the grids Vj used to round
the standard-deviation estimates are finite, and Theorem F.2 computes the corresponding
smooth policies.
The fixed-horizon calculation uses a finite net of priors and backward induction in the
posterior log-odds state. It restricts the state to finite boxes and partitions the Gaussian
increments into finitely many intervals. Gaussian tail bounds control the omitted tails.
Finite weighted sums approximate the remaining expectations, and Bernstein polynomials
approximate the action-value functions on the state boxes. The proof of Theorem F.2 controls
these errors and computes the second- and third-order derivative bounds of the smooth
kernels.

22

The finite matrix game selects a mixture of the computed policies. The mixture chooses
one policy before round 1 and follows it throughout the experiment. Lemma F.4 extends
a fixed-horizon policy to the required main-phase horizon. Each budget threshold is the
first integer satisfying a finite list of monotone inequalities. Theorem G.4 combines these
calculations. During data collection, SMAS rounds the estimated standard deviations to VjT
and evaluates the kernels assigned to the resulting index.

9.3

Bayes Optimal Strategy

For Bayes optimality alone, the sequential policy used when three or more arms remain after
screening is unnecessary. If SbT = 2, the main phase uses the Neyman allocation for the
retained pair and the empirical-best recommendation. If SbT ≥ 3, it selects every retained
arm equally often.
On the event where the pilot mean estimate satisfies its concentration bound, three or
more retained arms require a triple near tie. Such mean vectors have prior mass of order
O (τT2 ), and their contribution vanishes after the Bayes regret is multiplied by T and the
screening bound is applied. The exact Bayes constant is therefore determined by mean vectors
with a near tie between the two largest-mean arms, as shown in Appendix H.

9.4

LAN Approaches in Existing Studies

Applying LAN to all arms clarifies the local decision problem, and in the two-armed case
it yields exact Neyman-allocation results√(Adusumilli, 2022, 2023). If every mean is parameterized from the outset as m + ua / n, the analysis contains only local alternatives.
An arm whose mean remains a fixed positive distance below the best mean is outside this
parameterization, so LAN or diffusion arguments alone cannot show that the arm is removed.
In our construction, large-deviation concentration bounds screen such suboptimal arms and
connect the problem over the full compact mean space to the local Gaussian game that
determines the exact constant.
For example, Lai (1987) and Adusumilli (2023) both study Bayes-optimal allocation for
cumulative regret, but they obtain different strategies. Lai (1987) combines local asymptotic
analysis with large-deviation bounds on the number of selections of suboptimal arms. These
bounds separate arms with fixed positive gaps from local alternatives and make the sampling
proportions assigned to the former converge to zero. By contrast, Adusumilli (2023) restricts
the parameter space to local alternatives whose mean differences are of order n−1/2 . Under
this restriction, no arm has a fixed positive gap, and the optimal strategy is different. Both
studies derive strategies that attain the corresponding lower bounds, including the leading
constants.1
Bandit lower bounds often begin with an exact change-of-measure identity (Lai & Robbins,
1985; Kaufmann et al., 2016). LAN expresses the corresponding likelihood-ratio comparison
under local alternatives, and Le Cam’s third lemma transfers limiting distributions between
1

Adusumilli (2023) formulates the local problem through a diffusion limit. Lai (1987) does not use this
formulation.

23

nearby experiments. Our lower bounds use these likelihood-ratio arguments in the local
multi-arm and pairwise comparisons.

9.5

Uniform Arm Selection for Bernoulli Outcomes

For Bernoulli outcomes, the strategy δ unif in Appendix H.4 omits variance estimation and
selects every retained arm equally often during the main phase. Proposition H.5 shows that
it attains the exact Bayes constant.

10

Conclusion

In this study, we developed an asymptotically minimax and Bayes optimal strategy for
the simple regret, with matching lower and upper bounds over the class of all adaptive
experiments and for every fixed finite number of arms K ≥ 2. The exact minimax constant
Γ(P ) is obtained by first letting the number of rounds in the finite sequential Gaussian games
tend to infinity, then letting the radius C of the parameter set [−C, C][K] tend to infinity,
and finally maximizing over the common mean of the tied arms. For every prior H satisfying
Assumption 7.1, the exact Bayes constant C Bayes (P , H) is governed by pairwise near ties
between the best and second-best arms. SMAS removes clearly suboptimal arms in a pilot
phase whose fraction of the budget converges to zero as T → ∞, estimates the outcome
variances from the pilot observations, and selects arms during the remaining rounds using a
sequential rule when at least three arms remain and plug-in Neyman allocation when two
arms remain.

24

Throughout the appendices we work with a collection of arm families P ∈ P (E) that is
fixed once and for all and, wherever a prior appears, with a prior H satisfying Assumption 7.1
that is likewise fixed. We accordingly abbreviate the exact Bayes constant C Bayes (P , H) of
Bayes
(5) by C Bayes , and its pairwise terms by Cab
.

B

Detailed Literature Review

The earliest BAI formulation appeared under the name ordinal optimization (Chen et al.,
2000; Glynn & Juneja, 2004), focusing on non-adaptive optimal designs via large-deviation
principles. That literature often assumes that an experimenter knows which arms to select
and how often to select them to attain optimality, which requires knowledge of the arms’
26

outcome distributions. Beginning in the 2010s, BAI was formulated by explicitly addressing
the estimation of the optimal sampling rule (Audibert et al., 2010; Bubeck et al., 2011).
BAI is typically studied in two settings: the fixed-confidence setting and the fixed-budget

setting. In the fixed-confidence setting, we first fix a target error probability Pµ a
bδT ̸= a∗µ ,
while the sample size T is left unspecified. The strategy selects arms until the probability
of misidentification is theoretically guaranteed to be below a pre-specified threshold. This
setting is closely related to sequential hypothesis testing. By
 contrast, fixed-budget BAI aims
δ
∗
to minimize the misidentification probability Pµ a
bT ̸= aµ or the simple regret RegretδT (µ)
given a fixed sample size T . In this study, we focus solely on the fixed-budget setting and
refer to it simply as BAI.
Performance measures and uncertainty evaluation In BAI, two main performance
metrics have been used: the misidentification probability Pµ a
bδT ̸= a∗µ and the simple regret
RegretδT (µ). They satisfy the following identity:
X

RegretδT (µ) =
∆µ (a)Pµ a
bδT = a ,
a∈[K]

where ∆µ (a) := µa∗µ − µa denotes the gap in expected outcomes between the best arm and
arm a.
Optimality under the probability of misidentification and the simple regret depends on
how uncertainty about the bandit instance Pµ is evaluated. Throughout, the collection of arm
families P is held fixed, so only the mean vector µ ∈ MK varies. We use three evaluation
criteria:
• Distribution-dependent analysis: Performance is evaluated at a fixed mean vector
µ ∈ MK , that is, at a fixed bandit instance Pµ .
• Minimax analysis: Performance is evaluated under the worst case over µ ∈ MK by
taking the supremum of the risk.
• Bayesian analysis: Performance is evaluated by averaging the risk over µ ∈ MK
against a prior H.
The last two criteria therefore range over the bandit model B (P ) of Section 3 for the fixed
collection of arm families P . Neither criterion optimizes or averages over the choice of P .
Distribution-dependent analysis In a distribution-dependent analysis of BAI, the
misidentification probability and
 the simple regret are evaluated on the exponential scale in
T , through T1 log Pµ a
bδT ̸= a∗µ and T1 log RegretδT (µ). At a fixed µ with a unique best arm
and positive gaps, the two logarithmic
 rates coincide. The identity above bounds theδ simple

δ
∗
regret below by ∆min (µ)Pµ a
bT ̸= aµ . It bounds the regret above by ∆max (µ)Pµ a
bT ̸= a∗µ .
Both gap constants are positive, finite, and independent of T . Applying T1 log to the two
bounds removes the gap constants. Thus, at the level of fixed-instance
logarithmic rates, it

suffices to study the misidentification probability Pµ a
bδT ̸= a∗µ . This equivalence of rates
fails when the gaps shrink with T , as they do under the criteria of this study.
27

Lower bounds for this probability have been developed by Kaufmann et al. (2014, 2016),
extending the classical bounds for regret minimization (Lai & Robbins, 1985; Burnetas &
Katehakis, 1996). Degenne (2023) asks whether a task admits a complexity, that is, an
error exponent attained at every instance by one algorithm, and shows two things: if such
a complexity exists it is the one determined by the static oracle proportions, of the kind
considered by Glynn & Juneja (2004), and for several fixed-budget tasks, including two-armed
Bernoulli BAI, no such complexity exists.
For two-armed Gaussian problems with known variances, Kaufmann et al. (2014, 2016)
show that Neyman allocation, under which the sampling proportions are proportional to the
standard deviations, is optimal. They also show that when outcomes follow a one-parameter
exponential family and the number of arms is two, uniform arm selection is nearly optimal.
When variances are unknown, Kato (2025) proves that for two-armed Gaussian problems with
unknown variances, Neyman allocation with adaptive variance estimation remains optimal
in a local regime where the mean gap is small, while Wang et al. (2024) establishes, for
two-armed Bernoulli problems, that uniform arm selection is undominated, in the sense that
no algorithm weakly improves on its fixed-instance error exponent at every instance and
strictly improves on it at some instance.
For two-armed bandits under more general settings, as well as for bandits with K ≥ 3
arms, the existence of optimal designs long remained unclear (Kaufmann, 2020). While
strategies that match the lower bounds have been identified in the fixed-confidence setting
(Garivier & Kaufmann, 2016), such strategies have not been found in the fixed-budget setting.
In this setting, there are various technical challenges, including the reverse Kullback–Leibler
(KL) divergence problem (Kaufmann, 2020). Kasy & Sautmann (2021) claims to resolve the
question by adapting top-two Thompson sampling, originally proposed for fixed-confidence
BAI by Russo (2020). However, Ariu et al. (2021) identifies a technical issue in the proof
and provides a counterexample based on a different lower bound from Carpentier & Locatelli
(2016). Subsequent work provides both negative and positive results. On the negative side,
Degenne (2023) rules out a single algorithm attaining the best fixed-instance error exponent
at every instance for several identification tasks. In two-armed Bernoulli A/B testing, Wang
et al. (2024) rules out an algorithm that matches uniform sampling everywhere and strictly
improves on it somewhere. See also the open problem posed by Qin (2022). On the positive
side, Imbens et al. (2025) shows that batched elimination designs can strictly dominate
completely randomized trials with three or more homoscedastic Gaussian arms, which makes
a nonadaptive design inadmissible under an efficiency-exponent criterion. That result is a
dominance result, not an impossibility result, and it points the same way as our K ≥ 3
analysis, in which the sampling rule is allowed to respond to every observation.
Minimax and Bayesian analysis This study focuses on minimax and Bayesian criteria.
These criteria assess performance under uncertainty about µ, and they use a different
quantifier order from the distribution-dependent criterion: the worst-case mean vector may
depend on the budget, and a prior may place mass near ties. The negative results recorded
above are therefore neither evaded nor contradicted here, because they concern a fixed instance
and an exponential scale, whereas the leading term of the criteria below is polynomial in T .
Under these criteria, misidentification probability and regret lead to different conclusions.

28

We begin by explaining the reason for this divergence. For simplicity, consider a sequence
(µT )T ∈N of two-armed mean vectors for which arm 1 is the best arm at every budget. We
suppose for this heuristic that the error probability obeys an exponential upper bound with
some constant C > 0, so that


The first two cases use that schematic exponential upper bound, while the third uses only
that a probability is at most√one. This calculation therefore gives a heuristic explanation of
why the scale ∆µT (2) = C1 / T governs the worst-case and Bayesian analyses, and it rests on
a schematic exponential bound rather than on a bound that holds for every strategy. It is not
by itself a reduction to local alternatives. The lower bounds of Sections 6 and 7 establish that
reduction rigorously, for the stated model and for the whole class of adaptive experiments.
Minimax rate-optimal designs for simple regret are given in Bubeck et al. (2011), whereas
Bayes rate-optimal designs are proposed by Komiyama et al. (2023). These results achieve
optimal convergence rates, but exact constant matching between upper and lower bounds
remains unresolved in general.
Our contribution addresses this gap. We derive tight minimax and Bayes lower bounds,
including exact constants, and construct a single adaptive design whose simple regret asymptotically attains these bounds. Table 1 summarizes existing results and our contributions.

C

Finite Sequential Gaussian Game and Finite-Grid
Duality

This appendix records an auxiliary sequential experiment, the finite sequential Gaussian
game, together with two decision-theoretic facts about its finite-grid restriction: a sufficiency
reduction to the reference posterior and a minimax duality. In contrast with a static Gaussian
game that fixes all arm selections before any observation, the sequential game below may
change the selected arm after every observation, which is the feature used when passing from
fixed to adaptive experiments. The duality uses the following classical minimax theorem in a
finite-dimensional form. No additional compactness argument is needed.
Theorem C.1 (Kneser–Fan minimax theorem). Let X be a nonempty compact convex subset
of a Hausdorff topological vector space and let Y be a nonempty convex subset of a vector
29

space, carrying no topology. Let f : X × Y → R be such that x 7→ f (x, y) is convex and lower
semicontinuous for every y ∈ Y , and y 7→ f (x, y) is concave for every x ∈ X. Then
inf sup f (x, y) = sup inf f (x, y),

x∈X y∈Y

x∈X

y∈Y

and the infimum on the left is attained.
The theorem is due to Kneser (1952) and Fan (1953). See also Le Cam & Lo Yang (2000).

Behavioral
policies are closed under randomization performed before the experiment. Let

π (i) i∈I be a countable family of behavioral policies, and select an index according to a
distribution on I before round 1. The policy that follows the selected π (i) is again behavioral.
The selected index is part of the external randomization and remains available throughout
the experiment. The game has perfect recall, so each round conditions on the entire past of
the selected policy. Thus, the mixture selects At from a distribution measurable with respect
to Ft−1 and the external randomization. Its terminal choice also has the form required in
this definition. This is the elementary direction of Kuhn’s equivalence between mixed and
behavioral strategies under perfect recall.

Sequential Gaussian Game Value and Bellman Recursion

This appendix builds on the finite sequential Gaussian game of Appendix C. It establishes
the limit of GC
S,n (s) as the horizon goes to infinity for fixed C, then takes the supremum of
that limit over C, and derives the posterior Bellman recursion for the Bayes value under a
full-support prior. These results are used in the comparison arguments below. Throughout,
S ⊆ [K] is a nonempty arm set with |S| ≥ 2, s = (sa )a∈S ∈ (0, ∞)S , and the game, its risks

Proof. Fix q ≥ |S| and a horizon N ≥ q. Put mN = ⌊N/q⌋ ≥ 1 and cN = mN q/N ∈ (0, 1].
Use only the first mN q observations. Given a behavioral q-round policy πq , form an N -round
policy as follows. Each time πq selects an arm, the N -round policy selects that arm for
mN consecutive rounds and supplies the normalized aggregate to πq . During the remaining
N − mN q < q rounds, it selects an arbitrary fixed arm in S and discards the outcomes. Its
terminal recommendation is the recommendation of πq . Thus, it is a horizon-N behavioral
policy whose regret equals the regret of πq in the aggregated experiment. If the parameter in
the N -round game is u and the q-round policy selects arm a, then the aggregate has law

Lemma D.3 (Basic properties of the global sequential value). The compact finite-horizon

The Bayes posterior in this signal experiment solves the posterior diffusion for Πt defined
above, with its innovation Brownian motion. The observation filtration and the filtration
generated by the innovation process and the external randomization coincide: the innovation
is obtained by centering the observations with the posterior mean, while the observations
are recovered from the innovation equation. Thus, the admissible controls in the posterior
diffusion are the observation-based controls. Uniqueness in law therefore identifies the
posterior update given by Bayes’ formula above as the constant-action transition of that
37

diffusion. For h = 1/q, the normalized increment X =

the hidden point u(ℓ) .
Thus, the diffusion sampled at the grid times has the same controlled transition kernel and
terminal loss as the posterior-state formulation of the q-round Gaussian game. Any control
that is constant on the grid intervals induces, through its regular conditional action laws at
the grid times, a behavioral policy for this finite controlled Markov chain. Conditional on
an endpoint increment, the Brownian bridge over the interval is independent of the hidden
index. Any dependence of a later action on that bridge can therefore be reproduced by the
external randomization allowed for a behavioral policy. Conversely, every behavioral policy
of the q-round game defines such a piecewise-constant control.
The finite-horizon dynamic-programming argument of Lemma D.11 therefore gives equality
of the two Bayes values. If some coordinates of p are zero, they remain zero and the same
argument applies on the corresponding face of the simplex.

These constants bound the coefficients and their spatial Lipschitz constants and the terminal

are time-independent, and the running cost is zero. The controlled diffusion therefore satisfies
the assumptions of Jakobsen et al. (2019, Theorem 2.1). Apply that result to the maximization
problem with terminal reward −g. If V p (s) denotes the unrestricted minimum Bayes loss and

The constant is uniform in the initial posterior p and in s ∈ [w, w]S because C0hor and C1hor
are uniform.
The quantitative proof of the cited theorem gives, before setting its smoothing parameter
equal to q −1/4 , an upper bound of the form

C ε + q −1/2 + q −1 ε−3 .
We now specify how to obtain an upper bound for C. Repeat Propositions 2.1–2.3 and
Steps 1–4 in the proof of the cited theorem with the bounds C0hor and C1hor . The continuousdependence estimates for the diffusions follow from the L2 Doob inequality with constant 4,
Itô isometry, and Gronwall inequality.
38

For the regularization, use the product of the time kernel proportional to t5 (1 − t)5 1[0 <
5
t < 1] and the spatial kernels proportional to (1 − x2 ) 1[|x| < 1]. These kernels are C 4 ,
which is sufficient because only derivatives satisfying 2m + k ≤ 4 enter the proof. Their
normalization constants are rational. Rational upper bounds for the L1 -norms of the required
derivatives follow from the support volume and the sums of the absolute values of the
polynomial coefficients.
Gaussian moments have rational upper bounds, and the exponential in the Gronwall
bound has rational upper bounds from its Taylor series with a remainder bound. All suprema
over actions are finite maxima. Following the cited proof in order and replacing each generic
constant by a rational upper bound on the displayed right-hand side at that step yields a
rational upper bound for C from L, C0hor , and C1hor . We use three times this upper bound as
Ahor , because ε = q −1/4 makes every term in the preceding display at most q −1/4 .
Finite-grid minimax duality gives

p∈P([L])

Every finite-horizon value is at least maxp V p (s), and the upper bound converges to maxp V p (s)
as q → ∞. Hence, maxp V p (s) = inf r≥|S| GUS,r (s). This proves the claim.

The value of the game does not decrease when arms are added. Therefore, screening does
not increase the leading minimax constant: whenever at least two arms are retained, the
game played on the retained arms is no harder than the game played on all K arms, while a
single retained arm incurs no main-phase regret.

Let π ′ be a behavioral policy of the S ′ -game with horizon n. Construct a horizon-n
behavioral policy π of the S-game that runs an internal copy of π ′ . Whenever the copy
selects an arm a ∈ S, π selects a, consumes one actual arm selection, and feeds the observed
outcome to the copy. Whenever the copy selects an arm c ∈ S ′ \ S, π generates a surrogate
40

√
outcome from the law N (−C/ n, s2c ) using the pre-experiment randomization seed U of
Section 1.1, consumes no actual arm selection, and feeds the surrogate to the copy. Once
the copy has completed its n internal rounds, π selects arbitrary arms in S in the remaining
rounds and discards their outcomes until exactly n actual arm selections have been made, so
that π is a horizon-n policy of the S-game. The surrogate law is determined by C, n, and sc
alone, so every action of π is measurable with respect to the real observed history and the
seed, as Definition C.1 requires, and π never uses the unknown u.

admissible horizon-n policy of the S-game. The noise scalings agree

Definition D.1 (Global sequential minimax constant). Fix a true collection of arm families
P = (Pa )a∈[K] satisfying Definition 3.1, and write σa (m) for the standard deviation of arm a
at mean m. Define


m∈M

41

Since M is a compact interval and each σa is continuous on M by Definition 3.1, the
vectors (σa (m))a∈[K] range over a compact subset of (0, ∞)[K] . On this subset, G↑[K] is finite
by Lemma D.1 and multiplicatively continuous by Lemma D.3. Hence, the supremum is
finite and Γ(P ) < ∞. Although we write Γ(P ), the constant depends on the collection of
arm families only through the standard deviations evaluated at common mean values m ∈ M.
No other feature of the outcome distributions enters. The variance profiles that enter Γ(P )
describe the true environment and are not supplied to a strategy.

Remark. Lemma D.11 is stated for full-support priors, for which the log-odds chart and
the recursion are everywhere defined. The comparison arguments use it only for such priors.
When necessary, they approximate a boundary least-favorable prior from the interior. The
transition Ka,n is the Bayes posterior-predictive operator. The single-hidden-point operator
used in the comparison arguments is introduced where it is first needed.

E

Minimax Lower Bound

This appendix proves the minimax lower bound at the level of Γ (P ). The proof compares the
original local experiment with the sequential Gaussian experiment through posterior Bellman
recursions.

Remark. The order of limits is essential. We first fix (S, m, C, U) and all constants in
Lemmas E.3–E.8 and Theorem E.9, and then let T → ∞. At these fixed values, the transition
comparison constant C⋆ multiplies the uniform third-derivative bound A′ h−2 . The O T −1/6
finite-prior error therefore vanishes. Only after taking this limit do we let ρ ↓ 0, let C ↑ ∞,
and vary m. The arm set remains fixed at S = [K]. The bound applies to the class A of
all adaptive experiments because the reduction to policies that are Markov in the posterior is
performed after taking the infimum over behavioral policies.

F

Smooth Policies with Uniform Derivative Bounds

This appendix constructs, for the sequential Gaussian game of Appendix C, an indexed family
of finite-memory behavioral policies whose Gaussian continuation risks have second and third
derivatives bounded uniformly over the horizon. For every prescribed error, the quantitative
horizon bound and the finite calculations below give a finite threshold beyond which the
family contains policies whose worst-case Gaussian risks are within that error of gC
S (s).
The concluding lemma compares the risk of running one such policy on the original
experiment with its Gaussian risk. The additional terms account for the variance mismatch
and the third moments. These results are used in the upper-bound proof.
Throughout,

S (s),
G↑S (s) are those of Definition C.1 and Appendix D. The time-indexed game noise is written
Zt0 in this appendix, because Zt denotes the continuous state of a linear-state policy. All
vectors carry the Euclidean norm. For a bounded g ∈ C k Rd write M0 (g) = ∥g∥∞
and Mk (g) = max|α|=k ∥∂ α g∥∞ for k ≥ 1, and for a kernel α (· | z) into a finite set write
Mk (α) = maxa Mk (α (a | ·)).

ForPa hidden point ℓ and a time t, let µrt,ℓ be the law of zt under π r at u(ℓ) , and set
µt = ℓ µrt,ℓ , a finite measure on RL−1 . We approximate the Borel simplex-valued map
z 7→ αtr (· | z) in L1 (µt ) by a C ∞ simplex-valued map with bounded derivatives of every
order. First, fix a compact set carrying all but an arbitrarily small amount of µt -mass. Apply
Lusin’s theorem coordinatewise to obtain a closed subset on which the map is continuous
and whose complement has arbitrarily small measure (Folland, 2013). Apply the Tietze
extension theorem to extend each coordinate continuously to RL−1 without changing its
bounds (Folland, 2013). The resulting continuous map agrees with αtr outside a set of small
µt -measure. We project its values onto the probability simplex on S and then convolve
the projected map with a compactly supported C ∞ probability density. Projection and the
convexity of the simplex keep the map simplex-valued. A sufficiently small bandwidth makes
the L1 (µt ) error arbitrarily small, and convolution makes every partial derivative bounded.
Let αtsm denote the resulting action kernel. Applying the same construction to qqr under µq
gives the terminal kernel qqsm . These kernels define the linear-state policy π sm .
P
For probability vectors p and q on the finite set S, put ra = min {pa , qa } and r = a∈S ra .
With probability r, select the same arm from the probabilities ra /r when r > 0. On the
54

remaining event, use the two residual distributions. Their supports are disjoint. This coupling
disagrees with probability 1−r = ∥p − q∥TV , and all its probabilities are measurable functions
of p and q.
Use this coupling for π r and π sm together with shared Gaussian observations. At each
round, apply it to αtsm (· | zt ) and αtr (· | zt ). At the terminal time, apply it to qqsm (· | zq ) and
qqr (· | zq ). Let ς be the first time the two action sequences disagree, with ς = ∞ if they never
do. On {ς ≥ t} the two policies have played identically and, sharing the observations, occupy
the common state ztr of π r , whose unconditional law is µrt,ℓ ; conditionally on ztr the maximal
coupling makes the two actions at time t disagree with probability ∥αtsm (· | ztr ) − αtr (· | ztr )∥TV .
Hence, bounding the indicator of {ς ≥ t} by one,

For negative arguments, use Φ(x) = 1 − Φ(−x). Rational bounds for π follow from Machin’s
identity π/4 = 4 arctan(1/5) − arctan(1/239) and the alternating series for arctan. Taylor

bounds for logarithms of positive rationals. Square roots of positive rationals can be bounded
by bisection. Hence, all functions used below can be evaluated with rational lower and
upper bounds whose difference is at most any prescribed positive width after finitely many
arithmetic operations.

Thus, the state radii, Gaussian partitions, clipping ranges, Bernstein degrees, Gibbs temperature, prior net, and widths of the rational bounds in that proof can be chosen from q,

is a finite arithmetic operation, the computation of rational lower and upper bounds, or a
finite-dimensional linear program. Hence, the common refinement levels and constants are
obtained by a finite calculation.

G

SMAS and the Minimax Upper Bound

This appendix defines SMAS and proves that it attains Γ (P ) for every P ∈ P (E). The
strategy uses empirical means, empirical variances, and the seven constants in E.

G.1

Concentration Bounds for the Pilot Estimates

Recall the constants E = (v, v, Lv , λ0 , C0 , η0 , D0 ) in (1). Here, v and v bound the variance
functions, Lv bounds their absolute derivatives, and (λ0 , C0 ) and (η0 , D0 ) are the constants
in the two moment conditions. These seven constants are supplied to the experimenter,
determine the class P (E) in (2), and are the only information about the variance functions
used to construct the strategy.
Fix a first-stage size T0 divisible by K and put n0 = T0 /K. Stage 1 selects every arm
exactly n0 times and forms the empirical mean and the unbiased empirical variance

G.2

The SMAS Strategy

The strategy uses an integer index j ≥ 8. For every such j, set
αj = γj = j −2 ,

Bj = j 2 ,

Hj = j 3 ,

Rj = 8j 2 ,

ωj = j −4 ,

where ωj is the allowed excess risk in the Gaussian game. The relative tolerance rj for the
pilot variance estimates and the multiplicative grid spacing ζj for the rounded standard
deviations are fixed in Definition 5.1, after D2,j , D3,j , and B0,j have been fixed, at values in
(0, j −4 ] small enough that the resulting error is at most 2j −1 . The numerical values are not
optimized; they are chosen so that every error depending only on j in Theorem G.10 vanishes
as j → ∞. For each j, the parameter grids, the standard-deviation grids, and the derivative
bounds are finite, and the indexed policies use one set of bounds for all horizons. Theorem D.5,
Lemma G.3, and the comparison bounds of this appendix compute these constants, grids,
policies, and budget thresholds using only the constants in E and the quantities already fixed
for j.
The same budget thresholds must satisfy the requirements of the worst-case upper bound
in Theorem G.10 and the average upper bound in Theorem H.3, so we fix them once
before defining the strategy. Write Rmm
for the finite list of requirements in the proof of

for j ≥ 9,
(15)
which satisfies both lists for every j. The strict increment makes Nj strictly increasing, hence
Nj ↑ ∞; without it, a sequence of budget thresholds that was eventually constant would leave
the set below unbounded and jT undefined. For T ≥ N8 , the index selected for budget T is
jT = max {j ≥ 8 : Nj ≤ T } .
It is well defined and satisfies jT ≥ J for every T ≥ NJ , so jT → ∞ as T → ∞. For
the finitely many budgets T < N8 , the strategy selects arm 1 in every round and chooses
arm 1. This fixed Borel initialization does not affect the asymptotic results as T → ∞. No
growth rate for jT is imposed. The arguments below use only that jT → ∞ and that the
requirements of the two lists hold at the selected
√ index jT , which they do because T ≥ NjT
by the definition of jT ; a quantity such as Bj3T / T is therefore at most jT−1 not because jT
grows at any particular rate, but because that inequality is one of the requirements imposed
on Nj . Neither list involves the prior, and, by (3), neither involves the true arm families or
their variance functions, so (15) fixes one sequence of budget thresholds, and with it one
strategy, for every collection of arm families in the model class and every prior.

70

Theorem G.4 (Finite computation of SMAS). Suppose that K is given, that min M <
max M, and that the two endpoints of M can be supplied through rational lower and upper
bounds of any prescribed positive width. Suppose also that valid positive rational lower bounds
for v, λ0 , and η0 , and valid rational upper bounds for v, Lv , C0 , and D0 are given. For every
budget T , a finite calculation determines jT , the grids and indexed Gaussian-game policies
required at that budget, and the sampling and recommendation kernels of Algorithm 1. The
calculation uses finitely many arithmetic operations, rational lower and upper bounds, and
finite-dimensional linear programs.
Proof. Fix j ≥ 8. The quantities αj , γj , Bj , Hj , Rj , and ωj are explicit. For each of
the finitely many arm subsets, Lemma G.3 computes D2,j , D3,j , B0,j , d0,j , Cjcmp , and Nj0 .
Definition 5.1 then computes rj , ζj , and the finite grid Vj . For a supplied grid point and
main horizon, the same lemma computes the assigned policy.
Refine the endpoint bounds until they
√ give rational numbers 0 < ∆M ≤ ∆M ≤ ∆M . We
use ∆M in requirements of the form x/ T ≤ ∆M and ∆M in constants that are nondecreasing
in the diameter.
The constants in the concentration bounds for the pilot mean and variance estimates are
given by the formulas in Lemmas G.1 and G.2. The constants in the two-arm comparison are
given by Lemmas G.8, G.9, and I.3. The final-comparison bounds use the same pilot-mean
constant and elementary Gaussian integrals. Scalar quantities such as square roots, cmm ,
and Gaussian moments are replaced by rational lower or upper bounds in the required
direction; the bounds cmm ≤ 1 and E |Z|3 ≤ 2 already suffice. Thus, after the policies and
their derivative bounds have been computed, every requirement in Rmm
and RB
j
j is a finite
numerical inequality.
The construction preceding (15) defines Njmm and NjB by integer searches over explicit
nonincreasing upper bounds. Each search terminates because every bound converges to zero
as T → ∞ for fixed j. It then computes Nj . Starting at j = 8, compute Njmm , NjB , Nj , and
the policies indexed by j until the first index with Nj > T . Since Nj ≥ Nj−1 + 1, this occurs
after finitely many indices, and the preceding index gives jT when T ≥ N8 . The initialization
for T < N8 is explicit. Only the policies indexed by jT , the finitely many retained sets, the
finite grid VjT , and the realized main horizon are needed at budget T . Algorithm 1 then
gives the sampling and recommendation kernels by finite formulas.

where the last inequality follows from 1 + r ≤ 1 + r and 0 ≤ r, ζ ≤ 8−4 . Thus, the policy
is constructed using a standard-deviation vector sT whose relative difference from the true
standard deviations is at most ρjT , without evaluating the unknown variance functions.
Only this two-sided bound is used below. The upward rounding ensures that sT,a ≥ σa (µa ).
Therefore, the relative approximation error is nonnegative and the lower endpoint in (16) is 1.
The strategy reserves Tg = K⌊γjT T /K⌋ comparison observations and selects each arm
exactly Tg /K times during the corresponding rounds. These observations are interleaved
deterministically among the remaining rounds and are not used to update the state variables
of the main-phase policy. The remaining n = T − T0 − Tg rounds form the main phase, and
the action distribution is updated after each main observation.
For the analysis of δ SMAS , we use an equivalent representation with independent outcome
arrays for the pilot phase, the main phase, and the reserved comparison observations. For
each arm a, generate three mutually independent i.i.d. sequences

Conditional on the observed past and the seed U , the outcome of a round with At = a
has law Pa,µa under this construction exactly as under the single-array canonical construction.
Each arm selection uses an unused array entry that is independent of the past. Therefore, the
two constructions induce the same law of (A1 , Y1 , . . . , AT , YT , a
bT ). It follows that δ SMAS ∈ A
and its regret are unchanged.
Since the three arrays are independent and each phase uses a separate array, the comparison
sample is independent of the sigma-algebra generated by the pilot and main data conditional
on µ. Conditional on the pilot data FT0 , the main-phase observations from arm a are
conditionally independent with distribution Pa,µa . These are the two independence properties
used in the analysis below.

G.6

The Two-Arm Case

In the two-arm case, the sampling counts are determined from thep
rounded pilot standard deviations. The following lemma bounds the difference between cn = n (σa2 (µa )/Na + σb2 (µb )/Nb )
and σa (m) + σb (m). The term rj + ζj , which measures the relative error in the rounded
standard deviations, does not vanish as T → ∞ when j is fixed. Therefore, its coefficient
must be independent of j before we substitute the index jT selected for budget T . The lemma
is used in the minimax proof with m = µ⋆ and in the prior-averaged proof with m equal to
the midpoint of the two retained means.

Remark. The tuning depends on the model class only through the constants in E. Lemma F.6
compares the original and Gaussian one-observation transition operators at each round, so no
block average is conditioned on and no assignment sequence is fixed; the argument therefore
applies to the fully adaptive policy whose arm-selection probabilities respond to the observed
outcomes and that satisfies the bounds in Definition F.2. The two-arm case and the final
comparison rule are the only places where we analyze a choice rule defined by a discontinuous
comparison, and the two are controlled by different arguments: the two-arm case by the
standard-deviation approximation in Lemma G.8 together with the Berry–Esseen comparison
of Lemma G.9, and the final comparison rule by the exponential bound for the comparison
sample in Lemma G.7.

This appendix proves that the same strategy δ SMAS attains C Bayes for every prior satisfying
Assumption 7.1. The tuning is the same as in Appendix G.

H.2

Two Retained Arms with the Largest Means

On the event √
Eµ,T ∩ Ev,T (rj ), a true best arm is retained and every retained arm has mean gap
at most 4Bj / T by Lemma G.5. When exactly two arms are retained and they are the two
arms of largest mean, the two-arm case uses the plug-in Neyman proportions of Algorithm 1
and chooses the main empirical best. The following lemma evaluates the prior-averaged
contribution of these mean vectors.

with no exceptional set. Here n = T − T0 − Tg , and since T0 = K⌊αj T /K⌋ ≤ αj T and
