H.4

Uniform Arm Selection for Bernoulli Outcomes

Throughout this subsection, Pa,µ = Bernoulli(µ) for every arm. Thus, all p
arms share the
2
variance function σ (µ) = µ(1 − µ), and M = [µ, µ] ⊂ (0, 1). Write σ(µ) = µ(1 − µ) and
let Lσ = supµ∈M |σ ′ (µ)| < ∞ be its Lipschitz constant on the compact set M.
Let δ unif be the strategy that uses the pilot phase and the mean-screening set SbT of
Algorithm 1. It omits the pilot variance estimation, the standard-deviation rounding, and
the indexed policies of Definition 5.1. During the main phase, it selects each retained arm
either ⌊n/ SbT ⌋ or ⌈n/ SbT ⌉ times. The n − SbT ⌊n/ SbT ⌋ extra selections go to the retained
arms with the smallest indices. Hence, the sampling counts differ by at most one and are
deterministic given the pilot data. The strategy then chooses the arm with the largest
main-sample mean. It uses the comparison-observation schedule and the final comparison
rule of Algorithm 1 without change.
The tuning of δ unif uses information about the model class only through the mean space
M and the specified sequences αj , γj , Bj , Hj . The strategy uses none of the policies in
Definition 5.1, and its budget thresholds are defined explicitly in the proof of Proposition H.5.
Thus, δ unif ∈ A is a simpler finite procedure at each budget and uses only the observed
history.

Lemma I.1 (Score and Fisher information). Let {Pµ }µ∈M ∈ P(σ 2 , M, Y) and let ℓµ (y) be
the log-density of Pµ . Then, ℓµ (y) is three times continuously differentiable in µ and

Proof. Because Pµ is a canonical exponential family with natural parameter θ(µ) and logpartition b, the log-density is ℓµ (y) = yθ(µ) − b(θ(µ)) up to an additive term not depending
on µ. Differentiating and using the mean parameterization ḃ(θ(µ)) = µ yields

K.2

Shifted Alternative Pair and Gaussian Augmentation

The pairwise testing bound of Lemma K.6 below holds for every adaptive experiment δ ∈ A.
This subsection develops the tools.
Instead of comparing the two instances of the lemma directly, we compare the plus√instance
with a minus instance whose common level is shifted by an amount of order 1/ T . The
shift makes the two arms move by the same number of their own standard deviations. After
this equalization, every selection of either arm carries the same standardized information
about the comparison. Thus, the predictable quadratic variation of the martingale part of
the log-likelihood ratio has a deterministic pathwise upper bound for every adaptive arm
selection.
A conditionally Gaussian augmentation raises this quadratic variation to the deterministic
upper bound. The martingale central limit theorem for triangular arrays then identifies the
limiting testing affinity as the Gaussian affinity at that information level. Finally, a change
of variables in the tie-value integral maps the shifted comparison back to the original pair.
This step introduces the averaging over the tie value. The corresponding pointwise bound at
a fixed m is false, as noted in Remark K.3.
Throughout this subsection, fix distinct arms a ̸= b, a vector µ−ab ∈ int(M)K−2 , a scalar

under the plus augmentation and N (−gT /(2kT ), gT /kT ) under the minus augmentation; on

Proof. (1) The augmentation appends coordinates through Markov kernels acting on the
history. Hence, the history marginals are unchanged. Discarding the appended coordinates is
a common Markov kernel mapping the augmented pair to the original pair, and the testing
affinity does not decrease under a common Markov kernel. Equivalently, every test of the
original experiment is a test of the enlarged experiment that ignores the appended coordinates.
The infimum defining the affinity is therefore taken over a larger set of tests in the enlarged
experiment.
(2) On {gT > 0} the conditional log density ratio of ηj is, with v := gT /kT ,

The set of µ−ab having at least one coordinate on the boundary ∂M, which consists of the two
endpoints of the interval M, is a finite union of Lebesgue-null slices of MK−2 . We therefore

function ω is nonnegative and continuous on [α, β]. On this interval the indicator equals one,
and [α, β] ⊂ int(M).
If α ≥ β, the interval is empty and the corresponding contribution is zero. We therefore
assume α < β below. For K = 2, the indicator is identically one by the empty-maximum
convention of Lemma K.6. The outer integral over M0 is evaluation at its unique point. In
this case, fix α ∈ (inf M, β) and later let α ↓ inf M, together with β ↑ sup M and U ↑ ∞.
Since UT → ∞, only finitely many budgets satisfy UT < U , and they do not affect the limit
inferior. For the remaining budgets, restrict the u-integral to (0, U ] and the m-integral to
[α, β] ∩ M◦T . The integrand is nonnegative, so this restriction gives a lower bound.
When α < β, the interval [α, β] is a compact subset of int(M). Hence, [α, β] ⊂ M◦T for
all sufficiently large T because rT ↓ 0. The required threshold may depend on µ−ab , but
the limit inferior is taken pointwise in (u, µ−ab ) before integration over µ−ab . Beyond this

Lemma K.8 (Triple near-tie prior mass). Suppose that Assumption 7.1 holds. Then there
exists a finite constant Ctri such that for every r > 0,


H-probability at most Ctri r2 .

the parameter interval. If K = 2, there are no three distinct arms, the event in the statement

and define

The Three-Arm Numerical Comparison

This appendix supplies the two bounds used in Proposition A.1. Throughout, S = {1, 2, 3},
s = (1, 1, 1), and ∆◦ = {f ∈ (0, 1)3 : f1 + f2 + f3 = 1}.

M.1

The Limit Experiment

The evaluation is carried out in the n → ∞ limit of the game of Definition C.1. The game
realizes that limit exactly, rather than approximately, at every sampling-proportion vector
whose coordinates are multiples of 1/n. No finite game realizes a vector with an irrational
coordinate. Lemma M.2 therefore restricts attention to rational sampling proportions. The
realization is an identity between processes. We state it in this form because the corresponding
claim about a single marginal law is false: when the selection count of an arm is chosen
adaptively, the standardized sample mean of that arm is not standard normal.
Enlarge the probability space of the game to carry an array (Za,j )a∈S,j≥1 of independent
standard normal variables, independent of the external randomization, and let the jth
selection of arm a, when it occurs, use the noise variable Za,j . Array coordinates that
are not used by any arm selection do not affect the experiment. This relabeling preserves
the experiment because each At is measurable with respect to the past and the external
randomization while the round noise is independent of both, and it supplies the variables for
every index j, whether or not arm a is selected that often. Put
k

1 X
Za,j ,
Wa (k/n) = √
n j=1

k = 0, 1, 2, . . . ,

(42)

so that W1 , W2 , W3 are the restrictions to (1/n) Z≥0 of independent√standard Brownian
motions, independent of the external randomization. Because Xt = ua / n + sa Zt , after arm
a has been selected Na times, by whatever adaptive rule, its rescaled partial sum satisfies
1 X
√

M.2

A Lower Bound on the Static Value

Nature’s parameter matters only through its gap vector. Therefore, we may specify a prior
using finitely many parameter vectors, one from each class of vectors that differ only by
adding the same constant to all coordinates. The following lemma turns any such prior into
a lower bound on the static value. The point of the proof is that no equivariance has to
be assumed of the terminal rule: averaging the risk over the common location, and using
that an average never exceeds a supremum, produces the invariant Bayes risk up to an error
that vanishes as C → ∞, uniformly over vectors of sampling proportions and terminal choice
rules.

M.3

An Upper Bound on the Sequential Value

Any single policy bounds the sequential value from above. The next lemma records that a
policy of the limit experiment whose sampling proportions are rational is a policy of the
finite game of Definition C.1 exactly, with no rounding and no approximation argument, so
its worst-case risk bounds G↑S (s).
Lemma M.2 (Upper bound on the sequential value). Let m, q, q0 be positive integers, let
f 0 ∈ ∆◦ satisfy q0 fa0 ∈ Z for every a ∈ S, so that every arm has a positive sampling proportion
in stage 1, and set
Aq = {(i1 , i2 , i3 ) /q : ia ∈ Z≥0 , i1 + i2 + i3 = q} .
Let π be a policy of the limit experiment of Appendix M.1 whose first stage uses the increment
f 0 /m, whose stage-k increment for 2 ≤ k ≤ m is A(k) /m with A(k) ∈ Aq selected measurably
with respect to the past, and which chooses an arm attaining maxa∈S u
ba . Then
G↑S (s) ≤ sup R∞ (u, π).
u∈RS

Proof. Let n be any multiple of q0 qm with n ≥ |S|. At stage 1, π uses the selection proportion
fa0 /m for arm a, and nfa0 /m = (n/(q0 m)) (q0 fa0 ) is an integer.
 For2 ≤ k ≤ m, stage k uses
(k)
(k)
(k)
the selection proportion Aa /m, and nAa /m = (n/(qm)) qAa
is an integer. Define a
(k)

behavioral policy πn of the finite game by selecting, in stage k, each arm a exactly nAa /m
times, in any fixed order, with A(1) = f 0 and with A(k) chosen as π chooses it at the
corresponding state. Every action is measurable with respect to the past, so πn is a behavioral
policy in the sense of Definition C.1. It chooses an arm attaining the largest rescaled pooled
sample mean.
116

Couple the two experiments. On the probability space of the finite game, form W1 , W2 , W3
on the lattice (1/n) Z≥0 by (42), and extend each to a process on [0, 1] by inserting on every
interval [(k − 1)/n, k/n] an independent standard Brownian bridge between the two lattice
values, the bridges being generated from further randomness independent across arms and
intervals and independent of everything already introduced. Each extended process is a
standard Brownian motion, and its restriction to the lattice is (42). Run π in the limit
experiment driven by these Brownian motions. Every cumulative sampling proportion used
by π lies on the lattice, as verified next, so π does not use the bridge values. The cumulative
(k)
selection proportion of arm a after any stage of π is fa0 /m plus a sum of terms Aa /m, hence
of the form

πn equals ua Fa + sa Wa (Fa ) at each such time. Hence, the rescaled pooled sample mean of
arm a equals ua + sa Wa (Fa ) /Fa , which is exactly the statistic (44) used by π. Arguing by
induction on the stage index, the two policies observe identical statistics and therefore select
identical increments at every stage. Consequently, their cumulative sampling proportions,
their terminal statistics, and their choices coincide almost surely. Hence

The Two-Stage Policy

Let π two denote the policy used in Proposition A.1. It first selects each arm with sampling

This policy satisfies the conditions of Lemma M.2. In the staged formulation, take
m = 10, q = 6, and q0 = 3. During the first six stages, use the sampling-proportion vector

retained arm. Both vectors belong to A6 . The first six stages give proportion 1/5 to every
arm, and the last four stages give an additional proportion 1/5 to each retained arm.

then

Proof. Let arm 1 be best, so its gaps from arms 2 and 3 are x and y. First suppose that
x < y/2. If arm 3 is removed, the policy compares arms 1 and 2 after selecting each with
final proportion F = 2/5. The regret on this event is at most
 p

.
λf
If arm 3 is retained, then either its first-stage pooled mean exceeds that of arm 1 or it exceeds
that of arm 2. These probabilities are Φ (−λp y) and Φ (−λp (y − x)). Since y − x > y/2, the
regret on this event is at most the sum of the last two terms in the first display.
Now suppose that x ≥ y/2. If arm 1 is retained and arm 2 is chosen, the final pooled mean
of arm 2 exceeds that of arm 1. This event has probability at most Φ (−λf x) ≤ Φ (−λf y/2).
The corresponding probability for arm 3 is at most Φ (−λf y). If arm 1 is removed, then the
first-stage pooled mean of arm 3 exceeds that of arm 1, whose probability is Φ (−λp y). Every
loss is at most y, which gives the second display.
For y ≥ 14, every function of the form yΦ(−λy) appearing in Lemma M.3 is decreasing.

M.7

Finite Subdivision into Rectangles

The static calculation uses the square parameterization
f1 = r,

f2 = (1 − r) t,

f3 = (1 − r) (1 − t) ,

(r, t) ∈ [0, 1]2 .

On each rectangle, the extrema of fi , fi + fj , and fi + fk occur at its corners. We bound ρi (f )
by using the largest numerator and the smallest two denominator factors on the rectangle. The
bivariate normal distribution function is increasing in both thresholds and in its nonnegative
correlation. These bounds give an upper bound on each Pi (f ) and a lower bound on (49).
The recursive calculation examines 21,139 rectangles and gives
inf B(p, f ) ≥ 0.644.

f ∈∆◦

A finer numerical search gives the value 0.6451437085 at the equal sampling-proportion vector
(1/3, 1/3, 1/3).
For the calculation of the risk of π two , each rectangle gives intervals for δad + δbd , δad − δbd ,
and δab . We bound the two normal distribution functions in (50) uniformly over these intervals
119

and multiply the result by the largest loss on the rectangle. This gives an upper bound on the
risk throughout the rectangle. The recursive calculation examines 6,585 rectangles and gives
sup

R(x, y) ≤ 0.641.

(x,y)∈[0,14]2

A finer numerical search gives the value 0.6227561146 at gaps (1.9493485, 1.9493485). Lemma M.3
gives a smaller bound outside [0, 14]2 . Hence,

sup R∞ u, π two ≤ 0.641.
u∈RS

All Gaussian probabilities used in this calculation are bounded from below and above
using directed rounding. A rectangle in the static calculation is closed only when the resulting
lower bound proves the stated inequality. A rectangle in the calculation of π two is closed only
when the resulting upper bound proves the stated inequality. The rectangles cover [0, 1]2 for
the static calculation and [0, 14]2 for the two-stage policy. Hence, the displayed lower and
upper bounds hold uniformly over these two squares.
